<!-- conjecture-1: body_md -->
\label{siu-conj}
		\textit{Let $\pi:X\to\Delta$ be a smooth family of compact K\"ahler manifolds over
			the unit disk, with fiber $X_t$. Then, for every $m\geq1$, the $m$-genus $P_m(X_t)$ is independent of $t\in\Delta$.}
<!-- end conjecture-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{intro-main-criterion}
		Let $f:X\to Y$ be a proper flat morphism of complex spaces to a smooth Stein
		curve and $\mathscr F$ a coherent analytic sheaf on $X$ which is flat with respect to $f$. Fix $q\geq0$. Then the following conditions are equivalent:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(i)] $\mathscr F$ is cohomologically flat in dimension $q$ over
			$Y$.
			\item[(ii)] The base change maps in degrees $q$ and $q-1$,
			\[
			\varphi^j(y):(R^jf_*\mathscr F)_y
			\otimes_{\mathcal O_{Y,y}}\mathbb C(y)
			\longrightarrow H^j(X_y,\mathscr F_{(y)}),
			\qquad j=q,q-1,
			\]
			are isomorphisms for every $y\in Y$.
			\item[(iii)] The function
			\[
			y\longmapsto\dim H^q(X_y,\mathscr F_{(y)})
			\]
			is locally constant.
			\item[(iv)] The restriction maps
			\[
			r^j:H^j(X,\mathscr F)\longrightarrow
			H^j(X_y,\mathscr F_{(y)}),
			\qquad j=q,q-1,
			\]
			are surjective for every $y\in Y$.
			\item[(v)] The sheaves $R^qf_*\mathscr F$ and
			$R^{q+1}f_*\mathscr F$ are torsion free.
			\item[(vi)] The jumping classes satisfy
			\[
			J^{q+1}=0 \quad\text{and}\quad J^q=0.
			\]
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{intro-brill-noether}
		Let $B$ and $M$ be complex analytic spaces, $\pi:M\to B$ a flat morphism, and $\mathcal E$ a holomorphic vector bundle on $M$.
		Suppose that $B$ is Cohen--Macaulay and that for every $\max\{k-\dim B,0\}\leq i\leq k-1$,
		$R^{i}\pi_*(\mathcal{E})=0$ and
		$\operatorname{codim}_{B}(\operatorname{BN}_\pi^i)\geq k-i+1$ for the {i-th Brill--Noether locus} $\operatorname{BN}_\pi^i$. Then
		$R^k\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$ is torsion free.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{intro-threefold-plurigenera}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a $\mathbb{Q}$-factorial K\"ahler family of compact threefolds over a
		relatively compact, smooth, connected curve. Assume that $X_0$ has
		canonical singularities. Then, for every integer $m\geq1$, the $m$-th
		plurigenus $P_m(X_t)$
		is independent of $t$ near $0$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{intro-maxAlbdim}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a smooth K\"ahler family over a relatively compact,
		smooth, connected curve, and assume that $X_0$ has maximal Albanese
		dimension. Then, for every $m\geq1$, the $m$-th plurigenus $P_m(X_t)$ is independent of $t$
		near $0$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{intro-log-plurigenera}
		Let $g:X\to S$ be a smooth family such that $(X,B)$ is log smooth over a relatively compact, smooth, connected curve $S$. Suppose that the special fiber $(X_0,B_0)$ is projective with canonical singularities, that $\lfloor B\rfloor=0$, and that $K_{X_0}+B_0$ is big.
		Assume moreover that the negative part of its Boucksom--Zariski
		decomposition satisfies
		$$
		N(K_{X_0}+B_0)\wedge B_0 = 0 .
		$$
		Then there exists an open neighborhood $U\subset S$ of $0$ such that,
		for every integer $m\geq1$ such that $mB$ is integral, the $m$-th log plurigenus
		$P_m(X_t,B_t)$
		is independent of $t\in U$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{intro-higher-plurigenera}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a smooth family of compact K\"ahler manifolds over a
		relatively compact, smooth, connected curve. If $K_X$ is \emph{semipositive} (i.e., admits a smooth Hermitian metric with semipositive Chern curvature),
		then, for every $m\geq1$ and $q\geq0$, the higher cohomological plurigenus $P_m^{(q)}(X_t)$
		is constant on $S$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{intro-ginvarpluri}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a proper Moishezon morphism with connected fibers over
		a relatively compact, smooth, connected curve and
		$(X,\mathbf M)$ a generalized pair with abundant nef part over $S$. Write
		\[
		K_{X_t}+\mathbf M_t
		=\left.(K_X+\mathbf M_X)\right|_{X_t},
		\]
		and assume that $(X_t,\mathbf M_t)$ has generalized canonical
		singularities for every $t\in S$. Then, for every $m\geq1$ such that
		$m\mathbf M_X$ is Cartier, the generalized $m$-th plurigenera
		\[
		t\longmapsto
		\dim H^0\!\left(X_t,
		\mathcal O_{X_t}\bigl(m(K_{X_t}+\mathbf M_t)\bigr)\right)
		\]
		is constant on $S$.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
An $R$-module $P$ is called \emph{projective} if whenever $p$ is surjective and $h$ is any map, there exists a map $g$ such that the following diagram is commutative:
		\[\begin{tikzcd}
			& P & \\
			M & {N} & 0
			\arrow["g"',dashed,from=1-2, to=2-1]
			\arrow["h", from=1-2, to=2-2]
			\arrow["p"', two heads, from=2-1, to=2-2]
			\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3].
		\end{tikzcd}\]
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
Let $T:\mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{C}$ be an additive covariant functor between abelian categories, and suppose that $\mathcal{A}$ has enough projectives. For every object $A$, choose a projective resolution
		$$
		\textbf{P}=\cdots \to P_2 \to P_1 \to P_0 \to A \to 0.
		$$
		Taking homology, we define the \emph{left derived functors} by
		$$
		(L_nT)(A)=H_n(T\textbf{P}_A),
		$$
		where $\textbf{P}_A=\cdots\to P_2\to P_1\to P_0\to0$ denotes the
		associated chain complex.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
Recall that the functor $T(M)=M\otimes_R N$ is a right exact functor from $\mathbf{mod}$-$R$ to $\mathbf{Ab}$.
		Then its left derived functors are called \emph{torsion groups}:
		$$
		\operatorname{Tor}_n^R(M,N)=(L_nT)(M).
		$$
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
The \emph{projective dimension} of $M$, denoted by $\operatorname{pd}(M)$, is the minimum integer $n$ (if it exists) such that there is a resolution of $M$ by projective modules:
		$$
		0 \to P_n \to \cdots \to P_1 \to P_0 \to M \to 0.
		$$
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
For any integer $d \geq 0$, we have
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(i)]{\cite[Proposition~3.1]{Mat89}}\label{Prop 2.5}
			The $R$-module $M$ is free if and only if $M$ is projective.
			\item[(ii)]{\cite[Proposition~4.4.11]{Weibel94}}\label{pd-Tor-criterion}
			The projective dimension $\operatorname{pd}(M)\leq d$ if and only if $\operatorname{Tor}^R_{d+1}(M,k)=0$.  
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{2.7}
		The torsion group $\operatorname{Tor}^R_{1}(M,k)=0$ if and only if $M$ is a free $R$-module.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
If $\operatorname{Tor}^R_{1}(M,k)=0$, then $\operatorname{pd}(M)=0$. So $M$ has a projective
		resolution of length zero. Hence $M$ is projective and therefore free by
		Proposition~\ref{Prop 2.5}. Conversely, if $M$ is free, then it is
		projective, so $\operatorname{pd}(M)=0$ and $\operatorname{Tor}^R_1(M,k)=0$ by
		Proposition~\ref{pd-Tor-criterion}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{Torzero}
		Let $R$ be a principal ideal domain. Then
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(i)] $M$ is isomorphic to the direct sum of finitely many cyclic modules. More precisely,
			$$
			M \cong R^r \oplus R/(a_1) \oplus R/(a_2) \oplus \cdots \oplus R/(a_m)
			$$
			for some integer $r \geq 0$ and nonzero elements $a_1, a_2, \cdots, a_m$ of $R$ which satisfy the divisibility relations
			$$
			a_1 |a_2| \cdots |a_m.
			$$
			\item[(ii)] $M$ is torsion free if and only if $M$ is free.
			\item[(iii)] In the decomposition in (i),
			$$
			\operatorname{Tor}(M) \cong R/(a_1) \oplus R/(a_2) \oplus \cdots \oplus R/(a_m),
			$$
			where $\operatorname{Tor}(M):=\{x \in M\ |\ rx=0$ for some nonzero $r \in R\}$ is the \emph{torsion submodule} of $M$. 
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{UCT}
		For any cochain complex of free $R$-modules $C^\bullet$, there is a split exact sequence
		$$
		0 \to H^q(C^\bullet) \otimes_R M \to H^q(C^\bullet \otimes_R M) \to \operatorname{Tor}_1^R(H^{q+1}(C^\bullet),M) \to 0.
		$$
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
Let $(X,\mathcal{O}_X)$ be a reduced complex space. For every $\mathcal{O}_X$-module $\mathscr{S}$ we define 
		$$
		T(\mathscr{S}):= \bigcup_{x \in X} T(\mathscr{S}_x), 
		$$
		where $T(\mathscr{S}_x):=\{s_x \in \mathscr{S}_x: g_xs_x=0$ for a suitable nonzero divisor $g_x \in \mathcal{O}_{X,x}$\}. 
		We call $T(\mathscr{S})$ the \emph{torsion sheaf} of $\mathscr{S}$, and we
		say that $\mathscr{S}$ is \emph{torsion free} at $x$ if
		$T(\mathscr{S})_x=0$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{torsion-sheaf}
		Let $\mathscr{S}$ be a coherent analytic sheaf on $X$. Then $T(\mathscr{S})$ is the kernel of the natural $\mathcal{O}$-homomorphism $\sigma: \mathscr{S} \to \mathscr{S}^{**}$ of $\mathscr{S}$ into its bidual. In particular, $T(\mathscr{S})$ is coherent.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
For any coherent analytic sheaf $\mathscr{F}$ on a curve $X$, if $\mathscr{F}$ is torsion free at a point $x$, then it is torsion free over the dense Zariski open neighborhood of $x$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{torsion-sheaf}, 
		$T(\mathscr{F})$ is a coherent subsheaf of $\mathscr{F}$. Therefore, its
		support $\operatorname{Supp}(T(\mathscr{F}))$ is a closed analytic subset of $X$. Since
		$X$ is a smooth curve, a proper closed analytic subset is discrete. Because
		$(T(\mathscr{F}))_x=0$, the support is proper and does not contain $x$.
		Thus $U=X\setminus\operatorname{Supp}(T(\mathscr{F}))$ is a Zariski open dense
		neighborhood of $x$, and $\mathscr{F}$ is torsion free on $U$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{p-pushforward-torsion free}
		Let $X,Y$ be normal complex analytic varieties, $f:X\to Y$ a dominant holomorphic map,
		and $\mathscr{F}$ a torsion free coherent analytic sheaf on $X$. Then
		$f_*\mathscr{F}$ is torsion free.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Fix $y\in Y$. Suppose that $t\in(f_*\mathscr{F})_y$ and that there is a
		nonzero holomorphic germ $u\in\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}$ such that $u\cdot t=0$.
		We show that $t=0$. Choose a connected open neighborhood $V \subset Y$
		and a section $a\in \mathcal{O}_{Y}(V)$ whose germ at $y$ is $u$, such
		that $t$ is represented by a section
		\[
		s \in f_*\mathscr{F}(V)=\mathscr{F}(f^{-1}(V)).
		\]
		Then $u\cdot t=0$ means $(f^*a)\cdot s=0$ on $f^{-1}(V)$.
		Since $a$ is nonzero and $V$ is connected, the zero set $Z(a)$ is a proper analytic subset of $V$; in particular,
		\[
		\dim Z(a)<\dim Y.
		\]
		Now suppose that there exists a nonempty open subset $U \subset X$ such that $f(U) \subset Z(a)$. Then $f^*a=a \circ f=0$ on $U$. By the identity theorem, $a \circ f=0$ on $X$, i.e., $f(X) \subset Z(a)$, contradicting the dominance of $f$. Thus $f^*a=a\circ f \neq 0$ on every open subset of $f^{-1}(V)$. Since $X$ is normal, $\mathcal{O}_{X,x}$ is an integral domain for every $x \in X$. Hence $(f^*a)_x$ cannot be a zero divisor.
		If $(f^*a)_x\cdot s_x=0$, then $s_x=0$ for every $x\in f^{-1}(V)$, so $s=0$. Hence $t=0 \in (f_*\mathscr{F})_y$, which completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
Let $f:X \to Y$ be a continuous map of topological spaces and $\mathscr{F}$ a sheaf of abelian groups on $X$. Denote by $R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})$ the sheaf associated to the presheaf on $Y$
		$$
		V \longmapsto H^q(f^{-1}(V),\mathscr{F}),
		$$
		whose restrictions are naturally defined for any $q \geq 0$. The sheaves $R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})$ are called the \emph{higher direct images} of $\mathscr{F}$.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{higher stalk}
		Let $f: X \to Y$ be a proper map between locally compact spaces and $\mathscr{F}$ a sheaf of abelian groups on $X$. For any point $y \in Y$ and integer $q \geq 0$,
		$$
		(R^qf_*(\mathscr{F}))_y \simeq H^q(f^{-1}(y),\mathscr{F}).
		$$
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{direct image}
		Let $f:X \to Y$ be a proper morphism of complex spaces and $\mathscr{F}$ a coherent analytic sheaf on $X$. Then the analytic sheaves $R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})$ are coherent for any $q \geqslant 0$.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
Let $f: X \to Y$ be a morphism of complex spaces and $y \in Y$. We denote by $\mathfrak{m}_y$ both the maximal ideal of $\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}$ and the natural ideal sheaf defined by it; by $\hat{\mathfrak{m}}_y$ we mean the ideal sheaf of $\mathcal{O}_X$ generated by the inverse image of $\mathfrak{m}_y$. For any coherent analytic sheaf $\mathscr{F}$ on $X$, the quotient $$\mathscr{F}_{(y)}:= \mathscr{F}/\hat{\mathfrak{m}}_y \mathscr{F}$$ is called the \emph{analytic fiber} of $\mathscr{F}$ over $y$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{base change}
		Let $f:X \to Y$ be a proper morphism of complex spaces, $\mathscr{F}$ a coherent analytic sheaf on $X$ which is flat with respect to $f$, $y$ a point in $Y$ and $q$ an integer. The following assertions are equivalent:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(a)] For an arbitrary $\mathcal{O}_y$-module $M$ of finite type, the canonical morphism 
			$$R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})_y \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}} M \to R^qf_*(\mathscr{F} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_X}f^*M)_y
			$$ 
			is an isomorphism.
			\item[(b)] The canonical morphism 
			$$
			R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})_y \to R^qf_*(\mathscr{F}/\hat{\mathfrak{m}}_y\mathscr{F})_y
			$$
			is surjective.
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{iso-sur-lem}
		In the setting of Theorem \ref{base change}, if the base change map
		$$
		\varphi^q(y):(R^qf_*\mathscr{F})_y\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}}\mathbb{C}(y) \to H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})
		$$
		is surjective, then it is an isomorphism.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
The canonical map in Theorem \ref{base change}.(b) factors as the natural map followed by the base change map:
		$$
		R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})_y \twoheadrightarrow R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})_y \otimes \mathbb{C}(y) \xrightarrow{\varphi^q(y)} R^qf_*(\mathscr{F}/\hat{\mathfrak{m}}_y\mathscr{F})_y \cong H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)}).
		$$
		Since the natural map is surjective, $\varphi^q(y)$ is surjective if and only if the canonical map $R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})_y \to R^qf_*(\mathscr{F}/\hat{\mathfrak{m}}_y\mathscr{F})_y$ is surjective. Therefore by Theorem \ref{base change}, if the base change map $\varphi^q(y)$ is surjective, then it is an isomorphism.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
For a ring $A$, the \emph{height} of a prime ideal $\mathfrak p$, denoted by $\operatorname{ht}(\mathfrak p)$, is the supremum of all integers $n$ such that there exists a chain $\mathfrak p_0 \subset \mathfrak p_1 \subset \cdots \subset \mathfrak p_n=\mathfrak p$ of distinct prime ideals.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
Let $R$ be a ring, $M$ an $R$-module and $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_r$ a sequence of elements of $R$. We say $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_r$ is an \emph{$M$-regular sequence} if the following conditions are satisfied:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(a)] for each $1 \leq i \leq r$, $x_i$ is not a zero-divisor on $M/(x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_{i-1})M$ and
			\item[(b)] $M \neq (x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_r)M$.
		\end{itemize}
		When all $x_i$ belong to an ideal $I$, we say $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_r$ is an \emph{$M$-regular sequence in $I$}. If, moreover, there is no $b \in I$ such that $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_r,b$ is \emph{$M$-regular}, then $x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_r$ is said to be a \emph{maximal $M$-regular sequence in $I$}.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
For a local ring $R$ with maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$, if $M \neq 0$ is a finitely generated $R$-module and $(x_1, x_2, \cdots, x_r) \subset \mathfrak{m}$, condition $(b)$ is satisfied automatically by Nakayama's lemma.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
Let $(R,\mathfrak{m},k)$ be a Noetherian local ring and $I$ a proper ideal of $R$. We call the common length of the maximal $R$-sequences in $I$ the \emph{grade} of $I$, denoted by $\operatorname{grade}(I)$. In particular, we call the grade of $\mathfrak{m}$ the \emph{depth} of $R$, denoted by depth$(R)$. We say that $R$ is a \emph{Cohen--Macaulay ring} if depth$(R)=\dim R$.
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{CM}
		If $R$ is a Cohen--Macaulay ring and $I$ is a proper ideal of $R$, then
		$\operatorname{grade}(I)=\operatorname{ht}(I)$ and
		$\operatorname{ht}(I)+\dim(R/I)=\dim R$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
A \emph{log pair} $(X,B)$ consists of a normal complex analytic variety $X$ and an effective $\mathbb{R}$-Weil divisor $B$ such that $K_X+B$ is $\mathbb{R}$-Cartier. Here $K_X$ denotes the canonical sheaf $\omega_X$ in divisor notation. If the coefficients of $B$ lie in $[0,1]$, then $B$ is called a \emph{boundary}.
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- definition-12: body_md -->
Let $f:X\to S$ be a proper surjective morphism of complex analytic varieties, with $X$ normal and $S$ relatively compact. An \emph{analytic generalized pair} $(X/S,B+\boldsymbol{\beta})$ consists of an effective $\mathbb{R}$-Weil divisor $B$ and a positive closed real $b$-$(1,1)$-current $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ for which there exist a resolution $\nu:X'\to X$ and an $\mathbb{R}$-divisor $B'$ on $X'$ satisfying:
		\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
			\item $B'$ has simple normal crossing support and $\nu_*B'=B$;
			\item $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ descends to $X'$, and its trace $\boldsymbol{\beta}_{X'}$ has local potentials with $[\boldsymbol{\beta}_{X'}]\in H^{1,1}_{\mathrm{BC}}(X',\mathbb{R})$ nef over $S$;
			\item there is a class $\gamma\in H^{1,1}_{\mathrm{BC}}(X,\mathbb{R})$ such that
			\[
			[K_{X'}+B'+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{X'}]=\nu^*\gamma.
			\]
		\end{enumerate}
		Here a \emph{positive closed real $b$-$(1,1)$-current} is a collection of positive closed real $(1,1)$-currents $\boldsymbol{\beta}_Y$ on the proper bimeromorphic models $Y\to X$, compatible with pushforward: $p_*\boldsymbol{\beta}_{Y_1}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{Y_2}$ for every morphism $p:Y_1\to Y_2$ over $X$. The current $\boldsymbol{\beta}_Y$ is called its \emph{trace} on $Y$. \emph{Descent} to $X'$ means that $\boldsymbol{\beta}_{X'}$ has local potentials and
		\[
		\boldsymbol{\beta}_Y=\mu^*\boldsymbol{\beta}_{X'}
		\]
		for every higher model $\mu:Y\to X'$. We call $\gamma$ the \emph{adjoint class} and denote it by $[K_X+B+\boldsymbol{\beta}_X]$. If $X$ is K\"ahler, the pair $(X/S,B+\boldsymbol{\beta}_X)$ is called a \emph{generalized K\"ahler pair}.
		
		In the $\mathbb Q$-divisorial setting, we replace $\boldsymbol{\beta}$ by a
		$b$-$\mathbb Q$-Cartier $b$-divisor $\mathbf M$ and write
		$(X/S,B+\mathbf M)$. More precisely, a \emph{$\mathbb Q$-divisorial
			generalized pair} consists of an effective $\mathbb Q$-Weil divisor $B$
		and a $b$-divisor $\mathbf M$ descending to a resolution $\nu:X'\to X$,
		whose trace $\mathbf M_{X'}$ is $\mathbb Q$-Cartier and nef over $S$,
		such that $K_X+B+\mathbf M_X$ is $\mathbb Q$-Cartier. Descent means that
		$\mathbf M_Y=\mu^*\mathbf M_{X'}$ for every higher model $\mu:Y\to X'$.
		The divisor $B'$ is determined by the equality
		\[
		K_{X'}+B'+\mathbf M_{X'}=\nu^*(K_X+B+\mathbf M_X),
		\]
		and we choose $X'$ so that $B'$ has simple normal crossing support.
<!-- end definition-12 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{ExtendCont}
		Let $X\to S$ be a proper flat family over a smooth, connected, relatively compact
		curve and $\phi_0:X_0\to X_0'$ a proper surjective morphism of normal
		compact complex analytic spaces. Assume that
		$(\phi_0)_*\mathcal{O}_{X_0}=\mathcal{O}_{X_0'}$ and
		$R^1(\phi_0)_*\mathcal{O}_{X_0}=0$. If $f:X\to S$ is a small deformation
		of $X_0$, then $\phi_0$ can be extended to a contraction morphism
		$\phi:X\to X'$ over $S$:
		\[\begin{tikzcd}
			{X_0} && X \\
			& {X'_0} & {} & {X'} \\
			{\{0\}} && {S}
			\arrow[hook, from=1-1, to=1-3]
			\arrow["{\phi_0}", from=1-1, to=2-2]
			\arrow[from=1-1, to=3-1]
			\arrow["\phi", from=1-3, to=2-4]
			\arrow[from=1-3, to=3-3]
			\arrow[hook, no head, from=2-2, to=2-3]
			\arrow[from=2-2, to=3-1]
			\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-4]
			\arrow[from=2-4, to=3-3]
			\arrow[hook, from=3-1, to=3-3]. 
		\end{tikzcd}\]
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Although this result was proved in \cite[Proposition~(11.4)]{KM92}, we include a detailed proof. By Grauert's Kuranishi theorem \cite[Hauptsatz, p.~140]{Grauert74}, every compact complex space admits a versal deformation. We denote the corresponding spaces by $\text{Def}(X_0)$ and $\text{Def}(X_0')$, respectively.
		
		We first extend the contraction to the infinitesimal neighborhoods of $X_0$, and then to the formal neighborhood. Let $\mathcal{I}$ be the defining ideal sheaf of $X_0$ in $X$ and $nX_0$ the finite-order thickening $nX_0=(|X_0|,\mathcal{O}_X/\mathcal{I}^n)$. Since $S$ is a smooth curve and $f$ is flat, $X_0$ is Cartier; hence we have the exact sequence $$0\to \mathcal{O}_{X_0}(-jX_0)\to \mathcal{O}_{(j+1)X_0} \to \mathcal{O}_{jX_0}\to 0.$$
		After shrinking $S$ around $0$, the fiber $X_0$ is the principal Cartier divisor defined by the pullback of a local coordinate on $S$, so $X_0\sim 0$. Consequently,
		\[
		\mathcal{N}_{X_0/X}^{\vee}\cong\mathcal{O}_X(-X_0)|_{X_0}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X_0},
		\]
		and hence $\mathcal{O}_{X_0}(-jX_0)\cong\mathcal{O}_{X_0}$ for every $j\geq 1$. Thus
		\[
		R^1(\phi_0)_*\mathcal{O}_{X_0}(-jX_0)
		\cong R^1(\phi_0)_*\mathcal{O}_{X_0}=0.
		\]
		Applying $(\phi_0)_*$ to the preceding short exact sequence therefore yields
		\[
		0\to (\phi_0)_*\mathcal{O}_{X_0}(-jX_0)\to (\phi_0)_*\mathcal{O}_{(j+1)X_0}\to (\phi_0)_*\mathcal{O}_{jX_0}\to 0.\tag{*}
		\]
		Define $X_k' = \operatorname{Specan}((\phi_0)_*(\mathcal{O}_{kX_0}))$
		and let $\phi_k:kX_0\to X'_k$ be the induced morphism. Then
		$\phi_k$ restricts to the contraction $\phi_0$ on $X_0$, and $\phi_k$ are compatible when $k$ varies; thus $\phi_k$ is the
		$k$-th order infinitesimal thickening of $\phi_0$. The exact sequence $(*)$ gives a closed embedding $X_k' \hookrightarrow X_{k+1}'$, and these
		$X'_k$ for $k\ge 1$ are the infinitesimal thickenings of $X_0'$. Define a flat formal deformation $(X'_{\hat U},\mathcal{O}_{X'_{\hat U}})$ of $X'_0$ over the formal neighborhood $\hat U$ of $0$ in $S$, with $\mathcal{O}_{X'_{\hat U}}=\varprojlim_k\mathcal{O}_{X'_k}$.
		
		Next, we prove that the formal extension yields an analytic extension using the Douady space technique. Let $\pi': \mathcal{X}' \to \text{Def}(X_0')$ be a semi-universal deformation of $X_0'$, and consider $$f \times \pi': {X}\times \mathcal{X}'\to S \times \text{Def}(X_0').$$
		The relative Douady space $\mathscr{D} = \text{Douady}({X} \times \mathcal{X}' / S\times \text{Def}(X_0'))$ parametrizes compact complex subspaces of the fibers of $f\times\pi'$. Let $o=[\Gamma_{\phi_0}]\in\mathscr{D}$ be the point corresponding to the graph of $\phi_0:X_0\to X'_0$ and $\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{\phi_0}}$ an irreducible component containing $o$. Let $\Phi:\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{\phi_0}}\to S\times\text{Def}(X'_0)$ be the structure map of the relative Douady space, which sends $[\Gamma]$ to the pair $(s,t)$ such that $\Gamma\subset X_s\times\mathcal{X}'_t$. Let $\operatorname{pr}_1$ and $\operatorname{pr}_2$ be the projections from $S\times\text{Def}(X'_0)$ to $S$ and $\text{Def}(X'_0)$, respectively, and set $p_i:=\operatorname{pr}_i\circ\Phi$ for $i=1,2$, as shown in the diagram below.
		\begin{center}
			\begin{tikzcd}[ampersand replacement=\&]
				\&\& \mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{\phi_0}}\\
				S \&\& {S\times \text{Def}(X'_0)} \&\& {\text{Def}(X'_0)}
				\arrow["{p_1}"', from=1-3, to=2-1]
				\arrow["\Phi"{description}, from=1-3, to=2-3]
				\arrow["{p_2}", from=1-3, to=2-5]
				\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-1]
				\arrow[from=2-3, to=2-5].
			\end{tikzcd}
		\end{center}
		
		For a point $[\Gamma]\in\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{\phi_0}}$, set
		$s=p_1([\Gamma])$ and $t=p_2([\Gamma])$. Thus $[\Gamma]$ represents a compact complex subspace $\Gamma\subset X_s\times\mathcal{X}'_t$. We define the \emph{graph locus} $Z\subset\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{\phi_0}}$ by
		\[
		Z:=\left\{[\Gamma]:
		\operatorname{pr}_1|_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\longrightarrow X_s
		\text{ is an isomorphism}\right\}.
		\]
		After shrinking $\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{\phi_0}}$ around $o$, the subspaces whose first projection is an isomorphism form an open graph locus $Z\subset\mathscr{D}_{\Gamma_{\phi_0}}$ (cf. \cite[Theorem I.1.10]{Kol96}).
		Since $\Gamma_{\phi_0}$ is the graph of $\phi_0$, the projection $\Gamma_{\phi_0}\to X_0$ is an isomorphism, and hence $o\in Z$. For each $k$, the graph of $\phi_k$ determines a morphism from the $k$th infinitesimal neighborhood of $0$ in $S$ to $\mathscr{D}$. The compatibility of the morphisms $\phi_k$ makes these maps compatible as $k$ varies, so they define a formal section $\hat\sigma:\hat U\to\mathscr{D}$ of the projection to $S$, with $\hat\sigma(0)=o$. Since $Z$ is open and contains $o$, this formal section factors through $Z$. Applying Artin's theorem~\cite[Theorem~1.5]{Artin1}, we obtain an open neighborhood $U\subseteq S$ of $0$ and a holomorphic section $\sigma:U\to Z$ of the projection $p_1|_Z: Z \to S$ with $\sigma(0) = o$. Let $h=p_2\circ\sigma:U\to\operatorname{Def}(X'_0)$ and let
		\[
		f':X'_U:=U\times_{\operatorname{Def}(X'_0)}\mathcal{X}'\longrightarrow U
		\]
		be the pullback of the semi-universal family $\pi':\mathcal{X}'\to\operatorname{Def}(X'_0)$ along $h$. Write $X_U=f^{-1}(U)$. For each $u\in U$, the point $\sigma(u)\in Z$ represents a graph $\Gamma_u\subset X_u\times\mathcal{X}'_{h(u)}$. Thence $\sigma$ is holomorphic, these graphs form a holomorphic family over $U$ whose total space is a subspace
		\[
		\Gamma_U\subset X_U\times_U X'_U.
		\]
		The fiber of $\Gamma_U$ over each $u\in U$ is $\Gamma_u$.
		Denote the two projections by $q_1$ and $q_2$, as in the following commutative diagram
		\begin{center}
			\begin{tikzcd}[ampersand replacement=\&]
				\& {\Gamma_U} \\
				{X_U} \& {X_U\times_U X'_U} \& {X'_U}
				\arrow["{q_1}"', from=1-2, to=2-1]
				\arrow[hook, from=1-2, to=2-2]
				\arrow["{q_2}", from=1-2, to=2-3]
				\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-1]
				\arrow[from=2-2, to=2-3].
			\end{tikzcd}
		\end{center}
		Since $\sigma$ takes values in the graph locus $Z$, the first projection $q_1:\Gamma_U\to X_U$ is an isomorphism. Thus the second projection $q_2:\Gamma_U\to X'_U$ defines a holomorphic morphism over $U$ as
		\[
		\phi:=q_2\circ q_1^{-1}:X_U\longrightarrow X'_U.
		\]
		For each $s\in U$, its graph is precisely the subspace $\Gamma_s$ represented by $\sigma(s)$. In particular, $\sigma(0)=o=[\Gamma_{\phi_0}]$ implies $\phi|_{X_0}=\phi_0$. Thus $\phi$ is the required extension of $\phi_0$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
The result above does not hold without the condition
		$R^1(\phi_0)_*\mathcal O_{X_0}=0$. Consider the projection
		$\pi_2:A_1\times A_2\to A_2$ for abelian varieties $A_1$ and $A_2$; note
		that
		$$
		R^1(\pi_2)_*\mathcal{O}_{A_1\times A_2}\neq0,
		$$
		since $\dim H^1(A_1,\mathcal O_{A_1})=\dim A_1>0$. In this case, the
		first higher direct image
		$R^1(\pi_2)_*\mathcal O_{A_1\times A_2}$
		is torsion free. A general deformation of $A_1\times A_2$ is a simple abelian
		variety $\mathcal A$ and therefore admits no nontrivial morphism to a
		lower-dimensional abelian variety.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{ExtMMP}
		Let $g:X\to S$ be a flat proper morphism with connected fibers from a
		generalized pair $(X,B+\boldsymbol{\beta})$ to a smooth, connected, relatively compact
		curve. Fix $0\in S$ and assume that $\operatorname{Supp}(B)$ does not
		contain $X_0$. Suppose that $(X_0,B_0+\boldsymbol{\beta}_0)$ is K\"ahler and has
		canonical singularities, and that $\lfloor B_0\rfloor=0$, where
		$$
		\left.\left(K_X+X_0+B+\boldsymbol{\beta}_X\right)\right|_{X_0}=K_{X_0}+B_0+\boldsymbol{\beta}_0,
		$$
		and the negative part of the Boucksom--Zariski decomposition satisfies
		$$
		N\left(K_{X_0}+B_0+\boldsymbol{\beta}_0\right) \wedge B_0=0 .
		$$
		Then every sequence of transcendental MMP-steps
		
		$$
		\left(X_0, B_0+\boldsymbol{\beta}_0\right) \dashrightarrow\left(X_0^{(1)}, B_0^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_0^{(1)}\right) \dashrightarrow\left(X_0^{(2)}, B_0^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_0^{(2)}\right) \dashrightarrow \cdots
		$$
		extends to a sequence of $\left(K_X+B+\boldsymbol{\beta}_X\right)$-negative proper bimeromorphic maps
		$$
		(X, B+\boldsymbol{\beta}) / U \dashrightarrow \left(X^{(1)}, B^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(1)}\right) / U \dashrightarrow\left(X^{(2)}, B^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{(2)}\right) / U \dashrightarrow \cdots,
		$$
		over some open neighborhood $U \subset S$ of $0$.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- definition-13: body_md -->
Let $f:X \to Y$ be a proper morphism of complex spaces, $\mathscr{F}$ a coherent analytic sheaf on $X$ and $\mathcal{G}$ a coherent analytic sheaf on $Y$. Then the sheaf $\mathscr{F}$ is said to be \emph{transversal} on $\mathcal{G}$ if, for every $x \in X$,
		$$
		\operatorname{Tor}^{{\mathcal{O}}_{Y,y}}_i(\mathscr{F}_x,\mathcal{G}_y)=0, 
		$$
		for $i \geq 1, y=f(x)$.
<!-- end definition-13 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{main lemma}
		Let $f:X \to Y$ be a proper morphism of complex spaces, $\mathscr{F}$ a coherent analytic sheaf on $X$ and $y_0 \in Y$. Then there exist a neighborhood $V$ of $y_0$ and a complex $\mathcal{L}^\bullet$ of free sheaves of finite rank on $V$, which is bounded above, and which satisfies the following assertion:\par 
		For any coherent analytic sheaf $\mathcal{G}$ defined on an open subset of $Y$ such that $\mathscr{F}$ is transversal on it, there is an isomorphism
		$$
		R^\bullet f_*(\mathscr{F} \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_X} f^*(\mathcal{G})) \cong H^\bullet(\mathcal{L}^\bullet \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_Y} \mathcal{G})
		$$
		which is functorial in $\mathcal{G}$ and compatible with restrictions and
		short exact sequences in $\mathcal{G}$.\par
		Moreover, if $\mathscr{F}$ is flat over $Y$, then
		$\mathcal{L}^\bullet$ may be taken to be bounded.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{main-rem}
		If $\mathscr{F}$ is flat over $Y$, then $\mathscr{F}_x$ is a flat
		$\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}$-module and is therefore transversal to every
		$\mathcal{G}$. Applying Lemma~\ref{main lemma} to sheaves of the form
		$\mathcal{O}_Y/\mathfrak{m}_y\mathcal{O}_Y$, we obtain a bounded complex
		$\mathcal{L}^\bullet$ of finite-rank free $\mathcal{O}_Y$-modules such that
		$$
		R^\bullet f_*(\mathscr{F}) \cong H^\bullet(\mathcal{L}^\bullet) \quad \textit{and} \quad H^\bullet(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)}) \cong H^\bullet(\mathcal{L}^\bullet/\mathfrak{m}_y\mathcal{L}^\bullet) \cong H^\bullet(\mathcal{L}^\bullet \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}} \mathbb{C}(y))
		$$
		for every $y$ in the chosen neighborhood. Taking stalks at $y$ gives
		$$
		(R^\bullet f_*\mathscr{F})_y \cong  H^\bullet(L^\bullet) \quad \textit{and} \quad H^\bullet(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)}) \cong H^\bullet(L^\bullet \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}} \mathbb{C}(y))
		$$
		where $L^\bullet:=\mathcal L^\bullet_y$ is the complex of free $\mathcal O_{Y,y}$-modules formed by the stalks of $\mathcal L^\bullet$.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{jump-dimension}
		Let $f:X\to Y$ be a proper morphism of complex spaces where $Y$ is a smooth curve containing 0 and $\mathscr{F}$ a coherent analytic
		sheaf on $X$ that is flat over $Y$.
		Let $(\mathcal{O}_{Y,0},\mathfrak{m}=(t))$ be the local ring of germs of
		analytic functions at $0$, which is a discrete valuation ring with
		uniformizer $t$ and residue field
		$\mathbb{C}(0)=\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}/(t)=\mathbb{C}$. Set
		$\mathscr{F}_{(t)}:=\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_X}
		\mathcal{O}_{X_t}$. For $q\geq0$, write $\mathcal{F}^q:=R^qf_*\mathscr{F}$ and
		$h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)}):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}
		H^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})$. Set
		$b_q=h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})$ for nonzero $t\in Y$. Then:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(i)]\phantomsection\label{jump-cond-i} There is a noncanonical decomposition
			$$
			\mathcal{F}_0^q \cong \mathcal{O}^{\oplus b_q}_{Y,0} \oplus T^q, \quad T^q:=\operatorname{Tor}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y
					,0}}(\mathcal{F}^q_0) \cong \bigoplus_{i=1}^{\mu(T^q)}\mathcal{O}_{Y
				,0}/(t^{e_i}),
			$$
			where $\mu(T^q)=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(T^q \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}} k(0))$ and $\ell(T^q):= \sum_{i=1}^{\mu(T^q)}e_i.$
			\item[(ii)]\phantomsection\label{jump-cond-ii} The dimensions satisfy
			$$
			h^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})-h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})= \mu(T^q)+\mu(T^{q+1}).
			$$
			\item[(iii)]\phantomsection\label{jump-cond-iii} If moreover $\mathcal{F}^{q+1}$ is locally free at $0$ and the degeneration is simple (all elementary divisors $e_i=1$, equivalently $\mathfrak{m}T^q=0$), then
			$$
			T^{q} \cong (\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}/(t))^{\oplus(h^q(X_0)-h^q(X_t))}, \quad \ell(T^q)=\mu(T^q)=h^q(X_0)-h^q(X_t).
			$$
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
\hyperref[jump-cond-i]{(i)} Since $f$ is proper and $\mathscr{F}$ is coherent,
		Theorem~\ref{direct image} shows that $\mathcal{F}^q$ is a coherent
		$\mathcal{O}_{Y}$-module. At $0\in Y$, the stalk $\mathcal{F}^q_0$ is a
		finitely generated $\mathcal{O}_{Y
			,0}$-module. Since $\mathcal{O}_{Y
			,0}$ is a discrete valuation ring, it is a principal ideal domain. Thus, by Proposition \ref{Torzero}
		, $\mathcal{F}^q_0$ decomposes as
		$$
		\mathcal{F}^q_0 \cong \mathcal{O}_{Y,0}^{\oplus r} \oplus T^q.
		$$
		Since the torsion module $T^q$ is annihilated by some power of the maximal
		ideal $\mathfrak{m}$, its support is concentrated at the origin; thus
		$T^q=0$ on $Y^* = Y \setminus\{0\}$. By Theorem~\ref{base change},
		$r=b_q=h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})$ for general $t$.
		
		\hyperref[jump-cond-ii]{(ii)} Since $\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}$ is a discrete valuation ring,
		Theorem~\ref{UCT} and Remark~\ref{main-rem} give the exact sequence
		$$
		0 \to \mathcal{F}^q_0 \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}}k(0) \to H^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)}) \to \operatorname{Tor}^{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}}_1(\mathcal{F}^{q+1}_0,k(0)) \to 0.
		$$
		Taking dimensions in this exact sequence gives
		$$
		h^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})=\dim(\mathcal{F}^q_0 \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}}k(0))+\dim \operatorname{Tor}^{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}}_1(\mathcal{F}^{q+1}_0,k(0)).
		$$
		For $\dim(\mathcal{F}^q_0 \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}}k(0))$, since $T^q$ has $\mu(T^q)$ cyclic generators $\mathcal{O}_{Y
			,0}/(t^{e_i})$ and $\mathcal{O}_{Y
			,0}/(t^{e_i}) \otimes \mathcal{O}_{Y
			,0}/(t) \cong \mathbb{C}$, each $\mathcal{O}_{Y
			,0}/(t^{e_i})$ contributes one dimension. The free summand contributes
		$b_q$ dimensions, so the decomposition
		$\mathcal{F}_0^q \cong \mathcal{O}^{\oplus b_q}_{Y,0} \oplus T^q$ gives
		$$
		\dim(\mathcal{F}^q_0 \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}}k(0))=b_q+\mu(T^q).
		$$
		Similarly, we can get
		$$
		\dim \operatorname{Tor}^{\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}}_1(\mathcal{F}^{q+1}_0,k(0))=\mu(T^{q+1}).
		$$
		Consequently,
		$$
		h^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})=b_q+\mu(T^q)+\mu(T^{q+1}),
		$$
		and since $b_q=h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})$ for general $t$, we obtain
		$$
		h^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})-h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})=\mu(T^q)+\mu(T^{q+1}).
		$$
		\hyperref[jump-cond-iii]{(iii)} Since $\mathcal{F}^{q+1}$ is locally free at $0$, we have $T^{q+1}=0$;
		thus \hyperref[jump-cond-ii]{(ii)} gives
		$$
		h^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})-h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})=\mu(T^q).
		$$
		By \hyperref[jump-cond-i]{(i)}, 
		$$
		T^{q} \cong (\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}/(t))^{\mu(T^q)} \cong (\mathcal{O}_{Y,0}/(t))^{\oplus(h^q(X_0)-h^q(X_t))}.
		$$
		If the degeneration is simple, so that every elementary divisor satisfies
		$e_i=1$, then
		$$
		\ell(T^q):= \sum_{i=1}^{\mu(T^q)}e_i=\sum_{i=1}^{\mu(T^q)}1=\mu(T^q).
		$$
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
In particular, if we take $f$ to be a proper holomorphic submersion onto the unit disk, $\mathscr{F}:=\Omega(B_t)$ the sheaf of
		holomorphic sections of the smooth vector bundle $B_t$, then the dimension
		formula in \hyperref[jump-cond-ii]{(ii)} becomes the \emph{squeezing formula} in \cite[p. 68]{KS60}:
		$$
		h^{0,q}(t)+\nu^q(t)+\nu^{q-1}(t)=h^{0,q}(t_0),
		$$
		where $h^{0,q}(t)=\dim H^q(X_t,\Omega(B_t))$ and $\nu^q(t)$ is the
		number of eigenvalues of the Laplacian $\square_t$ that are smaller than a
		positive constant $c$.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{iso-sur-main}
		If $Y$ is Stein, then for any $y \in Y$, the base change map
		$$
		\varphi^q(y):(R^qf_*\mathscr{F})_y\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}}\mathbb{C}(y) \to H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})
		$$
		is an isomorphism if and only if the restriction map 
		$$
		r^q: H^q(X,\mathscr{F}) \to H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})
		$$
		is surjective.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
When $y \notin f(X)$, both sides are zero. Thus we may assume that
		$y\in f(X)$. Since $f$ is proper,
		Theorem~\ref{direct image} shows that $R^qf_*\mathscr{F}$ is coherent on
		$Y$. Since $Y$ is Stein, Cartan's Theorem~B gives
		$$
		H^p(Y,R^qf_*\mathscr{F})=0
		$$
		for all $p > 0$. Consider the Leray spectral sequence:
		$$
		E^{p,q}_2= H^p(Y,R^qf_*\mathscr{F}) \Rightarrow H^{p+q}(X,\mathscr{F}).
		$$
		Since all terms with $p>0$ vanish, the spectral sequence degenerates at the $E_2$ page. This induces a canonical isomorphism:
		$$
		\beta : H^0(Y,R^qf_*\mathscr{F}) \xrightarrow{\cong} H^q(X,\mathscr{F}).
		$$
		By Cartan's Theorem~A, the higher direct image sheaf $R^qf_*\mathscr{F}$ is globally generated on $Y$. Thus the evaluation map $\alpha_y$ in the following diagram is surjective:
		\[\begin{tikzcd}
			{H^0(Y,R^qf_*\mathscr{F})} && {(R^qf_*\mathscr{F})_y\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}}\mathbb{C}(y)} \\
			\\
			{H^q(X,\mathscr{F})} && {H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})}
			\arrow["{\alpha_y}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
			\arrow["\beta"', from=1-1, to=3-1]
			\arrow["{\varphi^q(y)}", from=1-3, to=3-3]
			\arrow["{r^q}"', from=3-1, to=3-3].
		\end{tikzcd}\]
		Therefore $\varphi^q(y)$ is surjective if and only if $r^q$ is
		surjective. Corollary~\ref{iso-sur-lem} now shows that $\varphi^q(y)$ is an
		isomorphism if and only if $r^q$ is surjective.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- definition-14: body_md -->
\label{coho-flat}
		We say $\mathscr F$ is \emph{cohomologically flat in dimension $q$ over $Y$} if the map $R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})_y \to R^qf_*(\mathscr{F}/\hat{\mathfrak{m}}_y\mathscr{F})_y$ is surjective and $R^qf_*(\mathscr{F})_y$ is a free module.
<!-- end definition-14 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{Main}\label{l-Extension}
		If $Y$ is a smooth Stein curve, then the following conditions are equivalent:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(i)]\phantomsection\label{main-cond-i}
			$\mathscr{F}$ is cohomologically flat in dimension $q$ over $Y$; see
			Definition~\ref{coho-flat}.
			\item[(ii)]\phantomsection\label{main-cond-ii} The base change maps
			$$
			\varphi^q(y):(R^qf_*\mathscr{F})_y\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}}\mathbb{C}(y) \to H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)}), 
			$$
			$$
			\varphi^{q-1}(y):(R^{q-1}f_*\mathscr{F})_y\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y,y}}\mathbb{C}(y) \to H^{q-1}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})
			$$
			are isomorphisms.
			\item[(iii)]\phantomsection\label{main-cond-iii} The function
			$$
			y \mapsto \dim H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})
			$$
			is locally constant.
			\item[(iv)]\phantomsection\label{main-cond-iv} The restriction maps  
			$$
			r^q: H^q(X,\mathscr{F}) \to H^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)}),
			$$
			$$
			r^{q-1}: H^{q-1}(X,\mathscr{F}) \to H^{q-1}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})
			$$
			are surjective.
			\item[(v)]\phantomsection\label{main-cond-v} The higher direct image sheaves $R^{q+1}f_*\mathscr{F}$ and $R^qf_*\mathscr{F}$ are torsion free.
			\item[(vi)]\phantomsection\label{main-cond-vi} The jumping classes satisfy
			$$
			J^{q+1}=0 \ \text{and}\ J^q=0.
			$$
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
\hyperref[main-cond-i]{(i)}$\iff$\hyperref[main-cond-ii]{(ii)} The
		equivalence follows from Definition~\ref{coho-flat} and
		Theorem~\ref{base change}.\par
		\hyperref[main-cond-i]{(i)}$\iff$\hyperref[main-cond-iii]{(iii)} This is just
		\cite[Theorem~4.12.(ii), Chapter~III]{BS76}.\par
		\hyperref[main-cond-iv]{(iv)}$\iff$\hyperref[main-cond-ii]{(ii)} This is
		just Proposition~\ref{iso-sur-main}.\par
		\hyperref[main-cond-v]{(v)}$\iff$\hyperref[main-cond-iii]{(iii)} If $h^q(X_y, \mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ is locally constant, by Proposition~\ref{jump-dimension}.\hyperref[jump-cond-ii]{(ii)}, we have
		$$
		\mu(T^q)+\mu(T^{q+1})=h^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})-h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})=0.
		$$
		But $\mu(T^q)\geq 0$ and $\mu(T^{q+1}) \geq 0$, thus
		$$
		\mu(T^q)=0 \quad \text{and} \quad \mu(T^{q+1})=0.
		$$
		Then by Nakayama's lemma, we have
		$$ 
		T^q=0 \quad \text{and} \quad T^{q+1}=0,
		$$
		i.e., $R^{q}f_*\mathscr{F}$ and $R^{q+1}f_*\mathscr{F}$ are torsion free by Proposition~\ref{jump-dimension}.\hyperref[jump-cond-i]{(i)}. Conversely, if these two sheaves are torsion free, then
		$T^q=T^{q+1}=0$ in Proposition~\ref{jump-dimension}.\hyperref[jump-cond-ii]{(ii)}, and hence
		$$
		h^q(X_0,\mathscr{F}_{(0)})=h^q(X_t,\mathscr{F}_{(t)})
		$$ 
		by the dimension formula above; hence $h^q(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ is
		locally constant.\par
		\hyperref[main-cond-vi]{(vi)}$\iff$\hyperref[main-cond-iii]{(iii)} By \cite[Theorems 1.1 and 4.2]{Gr65}, we have
		$$
		H^q(X_0)/E^q \cong J^{q+1} \quad\text{and}\quad H^q(X_t) \cong E^q/J^q.
		$$
		Taking dimensions gives
		$$
		\dim H^q(X_0)-\dim E^q=\dim J^{q+1}
		\quad\text{and}\quad
		\dim H^q(X_t)=\dim E^q-\dim J^q.
		$$
		Therefore
		$$
		\dim H^q(X_0)-\dim H^q(X_t)=\dim J^{q+1}+\dim J^q.
		$$
		If $J^{q+1}=0$ and $J^q=0$, then
		$$
		\dim H^q(X_0)=\dim H^q(X_t),
		$$
		so the function $y\mapsto\dim H^q(X_y)$ is locally constant. Conversely,
		if this function is locally constant, then
		$$
		\dim J^{q+1}+\dim J^q=0.
		$$
		Thus $J^{q+1}=0$ and $J^q=0$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem-main-criterion}
		\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
			\item\label{rem-main-criterion-a} The proof shows that no hypothesis beyond $Y$ being a complex space is required to deduce the remaining statements from \hyperref[main-cond-i]{(i)}. The implication \hyperref[main-cond-ii]{(ii)}$\Rightarrow$\hyperref[main-cond-i]{(i)} requires only that $Y$ be reduced; \hyperref[main-cond-iv]{(iv)}$\Rightarrow$\hyperref[main-cond-ii]{(ii)} requires only that $Y$ be Stein; and \hyperref[main-cond-v]{(v)}$\Rightarrow$\hyperref[main-cond-iii]{(iii)} requires only that $Y$ be a smooth one-dimensional complex space.
			\item An alternative direct proof of \hyperref[main-cond-iii]{(iii)}$\Leftrightarrow$\hyperref[main-cond-v]{(v)}, based on \cite[Proposition~4.12]{RT1}, is given in the appendix.
			\item 
			Theorem~\ref{Main} says that the following six conditions are equivalent.
			More precisely, its proof follows the directed paths displayed below; in
			particular, the converse implication from torsion freeness is obtained by
			first passing through $q$-invariance.
			\[
			\begin{tikzcd}[
				column sep=4.1em,
				row sep=3.6em,
				cells={nodes={
						font=\small,
						inner xsep=6pt,
						inner ysep=5pt,
						rounded corners=2.5pt,
						fill=white
				}},
				every arrow/.append style={
					line width=0.5pt,
					shorten <=3pt,
					shorten >=3pt
				}
				]
				|[draw=Gray!55, fill=Gray!7]| {\substack{\text{\hyperref[main-cond-iv]{(iv)} }q\text{-extension}\\(q-1)\text{-extension}}}
				& |[draw=Blue!70, fill=Blue!10, line width=0.7pt]| {\substack{\text{\hyperref[main-cond-ii]{(ii)} }q\text{-base change}\\(q-1)\text{-base change}}}
				& |[draw=Gray!55, fill=Gray!7]| {\text{\hyperref[main-cond-i]{(i)} }q\text{-flatness}}
				\\
				{}
				& |[draw=Blue!55, fill=Blue!5]| {\substack{\text{\hyperref[main-cond-v]{(v)} }(q+1)\text{-torsion freeness}\\q\text{-torsion freeness}}}
				& |[draw=Blue!70, fill=Blue!10, line width=0.7pt]| {\text{\hyperref[main-cond-iii]{(iii)} }q\text{-invariance}}
				\\
				{}
				& {}
				& |[draw=Gray!55, fill=Gray!7]| {\substack{\text{\hyperref[main-cond-vi]{(vi)} }(q+1)\text{-jump vanishing}\\q\text{-jump vanishing}}}
				\arrow[<->, shift left=5pt, "{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Prop.}\,\ref{iso-sur-main}}", from=1-1, to=1-2]
				\arrow[<->, shift right=5pt, from=1-1, to=1-2]
				\arrow[<->, "{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{Def.}+\mathrm{BC}}", from=1-2, to=1-3]
				\arrow[<->, "{\text{\cite{BS76}}}", from=1-3, to=2-3]
				\arrow[<->, shift left=5pt, from=1-2, to=2-2]
				\arrow[<->, shift right=5pt, "{\text{universal coefficient thm}}"', from=1-2, to=2-2]
				\arrow[<->,"{\ref{jump-dimension}\ \text{or}\ \text{\cite{RT1}}}", from=2-2, to=2-3]
				\arrow[<->, "{\text{\cite{Gr65}}}", from=2-3, to=3-3]
			\end{tikzcd}
			\]
		\end{enumerate}
		\noindent Here ``Def.+BC'' denotes Definition~\ref{coho-flat} together with Theorem~\ref{base change}. The upper route is justified by Proposition~\ref{iso-sur-main} and \cite[Theorem~4.12.(ii), Chapter~III]{BS76}. The lower route uses Proposition~\ref{jump-dimension}; the last edge uses \cite[Theorems~1.1 and~4.2]{Gr65}. An alternative direct proof of \hyperref[main-cond-iii]{(iii)}$\Leftrightarrow$\hyperref[main-cond-v]{(v)} is given in Appendix~\ref{app-base change}.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{l-squeeze}
		If $\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{q+1}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ and $\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{q-1}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ are constant, then $\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{q}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ is constant.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
By Theorem \ref{Main}, if $\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{q+1}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ is constant, $\varphi^{q+1}$ and $\varphi^q$ are isomorphisms. Similarly, if $\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{q-1}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ is constant, $\varphi^{q-1}$ and $\varphi^{q-2}$ are isomorphisms. Therefore, since $\varphi^{q}$ and $\varphi^{q-1}$ are isomorphisms, $\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^{q}(X_y,\mathscr{F}_{(y)})$ is constant.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{H}
		Let $f : X \to \Delta$ be a flat proper morphism from a normal complex analytic variety onto the unit disk in $\mathbb{C}$, and $L$ a line bundle on $X$. Then the following are equivalent:
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(a)] $L|_{X_t}$ is globally generated for any $t \in V$, where $V \subset \Delta$ is a nonempty Zariski open subset;
			\item[(b)] There exists a point $s_0 \in \Delta$ such that the base change map
			$$
			\varphi^0(s_0) : (f_*L)(s_0) \to H^0(X_{s_0}, L|_{X_{s_0}})
			$$
			is surjective, and $L|_{X_{s_0}}$ is globally generated. 
		\end{itemize}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
\label{egH}
		Let $C$ be a smooth projective curve of genus two and $\mathcal{P}$ the
		normalized \emph{Poincar\'e line bundle of degree two} on
		$C\times\operatorname{Pic}^2(C)$ (cf. \cite[p. 166]{ACGH85}). Choose a smooth analytic curve $S\subset\operatorname{Pic}^2(C)$ through
		$[K_C]$, together with an embedding
		$$
		\phi: S \hookrightarrow \operatorname{Pic}^2(C)
		$$
		such that $\phi(0)=[K_C]\in \operatorname{Pic}^2(C)$. It induces the map
		$$
		\Phi = \operatorname{id}_C \times \phi: C \times S \longrightarrow C \times \operatorname{Pic}^2(C).
		$$ 
		Set
		$X_s:=C\times\{s\}$. The map $\Phi$ gives the line bundle
		$$
		L=\Phi^*(\mathcal{P})
		$$
		on $C\times S$, with $L_s=L|_{X_s}\in\operatorname{Pic}^2(C)$, $L_0=K_C$, and
		$L_s\neq K_C$ for $s\neq0$. Since $C$ has genus two, it is hyperelliptic,
		and $K_C$ induces the map
		$$
		\phi_{K_C}: C \to \mathbb{P}^1.
		$$
		Thus $|K_C|$ is base point free; equivalently, $K_C$ is globally generated, and
		$$
		h^0(C,L_0)=h^0(C,K_C)=g=2.
		$$
		When $s\neq0$, the Riemann--Roch theorem gives
		$$
		h^0(C,L_s)-h^1(C,L_s)=\deg(L_s)+1-g=1.
		$$
		By Serre duality, we have
		$$
		h^1(C,L_s)=h^0\!\left(C,\mathcal O_C(K_C-L_s)\right).
		$$
		Since $\mathcal O_C(K_C-L_s)$ is nontrivial of degree $0$, it has no nontrivial global sections. Thus
		$$
		h^1(C,L_s)=h^0\!\left(C,\mathcal O_C(K_C-L_s)\right)=0.
		$$
		Therefore,
		$$
		h^0(C,L_s)=1.
		$$
		Thus every nonzero global section is a scalar multiple of a fixed section.
		Since $\deg(L_s)=2$, the zero divisor of this section contains two points
		(counted with multiplicity), which belong to the base locus $\operatorname{BS}(L_s)$.
		Hence $L_s$ is not globally generated for $s\neq0$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
Example \ref{egH} also shows that global generation of one restricted line bundle does not imply the local constancy of $h^0(X_s,L_s)$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{p-kollar-torsion freeness}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a proper K\"ahler morphism from a normal complex space
		with canonical singularities to a smooth complex curve. If $K_X$ is \emph{$f$-semiample}, i.e., 
		there exists an integer $r>0$ such that $rK_X$ is
		Cartier and the natural evaluation morphism
		\[
		f^*f_*\mathcal O_X(rK_X)\longrightarrow\mathcal O_X(rK_X)
		\]
		is surjective, then
		$R^i f_*\mathcal O_X(mK_X)$ is torsion free, and
		hence locally free on $S$, for all integers $i\ge 0,m\ge 1$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{l-codim-support-vanishing}
		Let $X$ be a locally irreducible complex analytic space and $\mathscr{F}$ a coherent analytic sheaf on $X$. If $\operatorname{codim}\operatorname{Supp}(\mathscr{F}) \geq 1$, then $\operatorname{Supp}(\mathscr{F}) \subset \operatorname{Supp}(T(\mathscr{F}))$. Furthermore, if $\mathscr{F}$ is torsion free, then $\mathscr{F}=0$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The support $Z=\operatorname{Supp}(\mathscr{F})$ is a closed analytic subset of $X$.
		If $\operatorname{codim} Z\geq1$, then for each $x\in Z$ we can choose a nonzero
		$f\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}$ that vanishes on the germ $Z_x$. By R\"uckert's
		Nullstellensatz \cite[p. 67]{GR84}, some power $f^k$ annihilates every
		element of $\mathscr{F}_x$. Since $\mathcal{O}_{X,x}$ is an integral domain,
		$f^k\neq0$, so every element of $\mathscr{F}_x$ is a torsion element. Hence
		$\operatorname{Supp}(\mathscr{F})\subset\operatorname{Supp}(T(\mathscr{F}))$. If $\mathscr{F}$ is
		torsion free, the latter support is empty, and therefore $\mathscr{F}=0$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{Teleman}
		Let $B,M$ be complex manifolds, $\pi:M\to B$ a proper surjective
		holomorphic submersion, and $\mathcal{E}$ a holomorphic vector bundle on
		$M$. Denote by $\mathcal{E}_x$ the restriction of $\mathcal{E}$ to the
		fiber $M_x$, regarded as a locally free sheaf on $M_x$. Define the
		\emph{Brill--Noether locus} by
		$$
		\operatorname{BN}=\{x\in B\ |\ h^0(M_x, \mathcal{E}_x)\neq 0\}.
		$$
		If $\pi_*(\mathcal{E})=0$ and $\operatorname{codim}_B(\operatorname{BN}) \geq 2$, then $R^1\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$ is torsion free.
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{Ex}
		Let $C$ be a smooth projective curve of genus two and
		$J=\mathrm{Pic}^0(C)$ its Jacobian. Write
		$$
		f : C \times J \longrightarrow J
		$$
		for the projection and $\mathcal{P}$ for a normalized Poincar\'e line bundle. Then $f$ is smooth projective and $J$ is a nonsingular projective surface. For $x \neq 0$, $L_x \neq \mathcal{O}_C$ is a divisor of degree $0$. It has no global section, thus $h^0(C,L_x)=0$. By Proposition \ref{p-pushforward-torsion free}, $f_*\mathcal{P}$ is torsion free; since it vanishes away from the origin, Lemma \ref{l-codim-support-vanishing} gives
		$$
		f_*\mathcal{P}=0.
		$$
		When $x=0$, $L_0=\mathcal{O}_C$ has only constant global sections, so $h^0(C,L_0)=1$. Thus the Brill--Noether locus contains only the single point $x=0$. Since $J$ is two-dimensional, $\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{BN})=2$. Then, by Proposition \ref{Teleman}, $R^1f_*\mathcal{P}$ is torsion free.
		By the Riemann--Roch theorem,
		$$
		h^0(C,L_0)-h^1(C,L_0)=\deg(L_0)+1-g=-1.
		$$
		Thus $h^1(C,L_0)=2$. Consider the base change map
		$$
		\varphi^0(0): (f_*\mathcal{P})\otimes k(0) \to H^0(C,L_0).
		$$
		Its source is zero, whereas the target is one-dimensional, so the map is not surjective.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rem-higher-direct-images}
		Since every fiber above is the one-dimensional curve $C$, Grothendieck's vanishing theorem (\cite[Theorem~2.7]{Hart77}) gives
		$$
		R^if_*\mathcal{P}=0
		$$
		for all $i \geq 2$. However, for higher-dimensional fibers, for example, let the base be $B=\operatorname{Pic}^1(C) \times \operatorname{Pic}^1(D)$, where $C,D$ are smooth projective curves of genera $g_1$ and $g_2$; then each fiber is the two-dimensional surface $X=C\times D$. Thus $R^2f_*\mathcal{P} \neq 0$, and the criterion above fails to establish its torsion freeness. We next generalize Proposition \ref{Teleman} to higher direct images using regular sequences, Cohen--Macaulay rings, and the idea of \emph{complete intersections} (cf. \cite[Chapter~II, Exercise~8.4]{Hart77}).
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- definition-15: body_md -->
We define the \emph{$k$-th Brill--Noether locus} of the pair $(\pi, \mathcal{E})$ as
		$$
		\operatorname{BN}^k_\pi(\mathcal E):=\{x\in B\mid h^k(\mathcal E_x)\neq0\}\subset B,
		$$
		where, for $x \in B$, $\mathcal{E}_x$ denotes the restriction of $\mathcal{E}$ to the fiber $M_x := \pi^{-1}(x)$ and $h^k(\mathcal{E}_x):=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}H^k(M_x,\mathcal{E}_x)$.
<!-- end definition-15 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{cri}
		Assume that $B$ is Cohen--Macaulay (e.g., $B$ has rational
		singularities; cf.\ \cite[p. 51]{KKMSD73}) and that, for every $k\geq 1$ and $i$ with 
		\[
		\max\{k-\dim B,0\}\leq i\leq k-1,
		\]
		we have $R^{i}\pi_*(\mathcal{E})=0$ and
		$\operatorname{codim}_{B}(\operatorname{BN}_\pi^i)\geq k-i+1$. Then
		$R^k\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$ is torsion free.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Fix $x\in B$ and suppose, for a contradiction, that $R^k\pi_*(\mathcal E)$ has torsion at $x$. By
		\cite[Proposition~7.1]{Mat89}, there is a nonzero element
		\[
		u\in (R^k\pi_*(\mathcal{E}))_x
		\]
		whose annihilator $\mathfrak p:=\operatorname{Ann}_R(u)$ is a prime ideal. Since $u$
		is a torsion element, $\mathfrak p\neq(0)$; set $h:=\operatorname{ht}(\mathfrak p)\geq1$.
		Let $I_{\operatorname{BN}}\subset R$ be the ideal defining the germ at $x$ of
		$\operatorname{BN}_\pi^{k-h}(\mathcal{E})$. The codimension hypothesis gives
		\[
		\operatorname{ht}(I_{\operatorname{BN}})\geq h+1>\operatorname{ht}(\mathfrak p),
		\]
		so $I_{\operatorname{BN}}\nsubseteq \mathfrak p$. Choose $f\in I_{\operatorname{BN}}\setminus \mathfrak p$. After localizing
		at $\mathfrak p$, the element $f$ becomes a unit, and hence
		$(I_{\operatorname{BN}})_{\mathfrak p}=R_{\mathfrak p}$. Thus the Brill--Noether locus is empty after localization
		at $\mathfrak p$. By \cite[Corollary~3.5, Chapter~III]{BS76}, the vanishing of $H^{k-h}(M_b,\mathcal E_b)$ at a point $b\in B$ implies that $R^{k-h}\pi_*\mathcal E$ vanishes on a neighborhood of $b$. Consequently,
		\[
		\operatorname{Supp}\bigl(R^{k-h}\pi_*\mathcal E\bigr)\subseteq
		\operatorname{BN}^{k-h}_\pi(\mathcal E).
		\]
		Since $I_{\operatorname{BN}}\nsubseteq \mathfrak p$, this support becomes empty after localization at $\mathfrak p$, and therefore
		\[
		\bigl((R^{k-h}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}))_x\bigr)_{\mathfrak p}=0.
		\]
		Since $\mathcal{O}_{B,x}$ is Cohen--Macaulay, there exists a regular
		sequence $(\varphi_1,\ldots,\varphi_h)$ in $\mathfrak p$, represented by holomorphic
		functions near $x$. Define $I_j=(\varphi_1,\ldots,\varphi_j)$ and
		$\mathcal{E}_j=\mathcal{E}/(\pi^*I_j)\mathcal{E}$. Then necessarily
		\[
		\bigl((R^{k-h}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_h))_x\bigr)_{\mathfrak p}=0.
		\]
		It therefore suffices to produce a nonzero element in this localized module;
		this will give the desired contradiction.
		\par
		Since $(\varphi_1,\ldots,\varphi_h)$ is a regular sequence,
		multiplication by $\varphi_j$ is injective on
		$\mathcal{O}_{B,x}/I_{j-1}$. Its cokernel gives the exact sequence
		$$
		0 \to \mathcal{O}_{B,x}/I_{j-1} \xrightarrow{\cdot \varphi_j} \mathcal{O}_{B,x}/I_{j-1} \to \mathcal{O}_{B,x}/I_j \to 0. 
		$$
		Since $\pi$ is flat and $\mathcal{E}$ is locally free, applying
		$-\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_B}\mathcal{E}$ to the sequence above gives the
		short exact sequence
		$$
		0 \to \mathcal{E}_{j-1} \xrightarrow{\varphi_j} \mathcal{E}_{j-1} \to \mathcal{E}_j \to 0.
		$$
		For $j=1$, the sequence
		$$
		0 \to \mathcal{E}_{0} \xrightarrow{\varphi_1} \mathcal{E}_{0} \to \mathcal{E}_1 \to 0,
		$$
		yields
		$$
		\cdots \to R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_0) \to R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_0) \to R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_1) \to R^{i+1}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_0) \to \cdots. \quad (1)
		$$
		When $\max\{{k-\dim B},0\}\leq i \leq k-2$, by our hypothesis, $$R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_0)=R^{i+1}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_0)=0.$$ 
		Thus $R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_1)=0$ for all $i \leq k-2$. Similarly, we have $R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_j)=0$ for all $i \leq k-j-1$.
		For the sequence (1) with $i=k-1$, we have
		$$0 \to (R^{k-1}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_1))_x \xrightarrow{\delta}(R^k\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_0))_x \xrightarrow{\varphi_1}(R^k\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_0))_x
		$$
		Since $\varphi_1\in \mathfrak p=\operatorname{Ann}(u)$, we have $\varphi_1u=0$ and hence
		$u\in\ker(\varphi_1)=\mathrm{im}(\delta)$. Thus there exists a unique
		$u_1\in(R^{k-1}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_1))_x$ such that $\delta(u_1)=u$.
		Since $\delta$ is injective, $\operatorname{Ann}(u_1)=\operatorname{Ann}(u)=\mathfrak p$. Repeating this process
		$h$ times produces an element
		$u_h\in(R^{k-h}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}_h))_x$ with $\operatorname{Ann}(u_h)=\mathfrak p$, completing
		the proof.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- example-3: body_md -->
Let $C$ be a smooth projective curve of genus $g_1\geq3$ and $D$ one of
		genus $g_2\geq3$. Then $B=\operatorname{Pic}^1(C)\times\operatorname{Pic}^1(D)$ is a torsor under the abelian variety $J(C)\times J(D)$ of
		dimension $g_1+g_2$. Let $M=C\times D\times B$ and $\pi:M\to B$ the
		proper holomorphic submersion; each fiber is the surface $X=C\times D$.
		Let $\mathcal{P}_C$ be the normalized Poincar\'e line bundle on
		$C\times\operatorname{Pic}^1(C)$ and $\mathcal{P}_D$ the corresponding bundle on
		$D\times\operatorname{Pic}^1(D)$, and
		set $\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{P}_C\boxtimes\mathcal{P}_D$. Then, for every
		$b=(L_1,L_2)\in B$, $\mathcal{E}_b=L_1\boxtimes L_2$. \par
		We apply Theorem~\ref{cri} with $k=2$. We first check the vanishing of
		$R^0\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$ and $R^1\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$. Decompose $\pi$ as $\pi_C: C \times \operatorname{Pic}^1(C) \to \operatorname{Pic}^1(C)$ and $\pi_D: D \times \operatorname{Pic}^1(D) \to \operatorname{Pic}^1(D)$. Denote the \emph{Brill--Noether variety} by
		$$
		W_d^r(C)=\{{L\in \operatorname{Pic}^d(C)|h^0(C,L)\geq r+1\}},
		$$
		By \cite[Chapter~5]{HM98}, we have
		$$
		\dim W_1^0(C)=1 \quad \text{and} \quad \dim W_1^0(D)=1.
		$$
		From 
		$$
		\operatorname{Supp}(R^0\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C)) \subset W_1^0(C),
		$$
		we know that $\operatorname{codim} \operatorname{Supp}(R^0\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C)) \geq \operatorname{codim} W_1^0(C)=g_1-1 \geq 2.$ And by Proposition \ref{p-pushforward-torsion free}, we know that $R^0\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C)$ is torsion free. Then by Lemma \ref{l-codim-support-vanishing}, we get
		$$
		R^0\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C)=0.
		$$
		That means
		$$
		\mathbb{R}\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C) \cong R^1\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C)[-1].
		$$
		Similarly, for curve $D$, we have
		$$
		\mathbb{R}\pi_{D*}(\mathcal{P}_D) \cong R^1\pi_{D*}(\mathcal{P}_D)[-1].
		$$
		Thus
		$$
		\mathbb{R}\pi_*(\mathcal{E}) \cong (R^1\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C)[-1]) \boxtimes^L (R^1\pi_{D*}(\mathcal{P}_D)[-1]) \cong (R^1\pi_{C*}(\mathcal{P}_C) \boxtimes^L R^1\pi_{D*}(\mathcal{P}_D))[-2].
		$$
		That means 
		$$
		R^0\pi_*(\mathcal{E})=0 \quad \text{and} \quad R^1\pi_*(\mathcal{E})=0.
		$$
		We now check the codimension conditions for $\operatorname{BN}^0_\pi$ and
		$\operatorname{BN}^1_\pi$. 
		When $i=0$, $h^0(X,\mathcal{E}_b) \neq 0$ if and only if
		$h^0(C,L_1)\geq 1$ and $h^0(D,L_2) \geq 1$. Thus
		$$
		\operatorname{BN}_\pi^0= W_1^0(C) \times W_1^0(D),
		$$ 
		which is of dimension two.
		Since $\dim B = g_1 +g_2$, 
		$$
		\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{BN}_\pi^0)=g_1+g_2-2 \geq 4>3=k-i+1. 
		$$
		When $i=1$, from
		$$
		H^1(X,\mathcal{E}_b)=(H^0(C,L_1)\otimes H^1(D,L_2)) \oplus (H^1(C,L_1)\otimes H^0(D,L_2)),
		$$
		we have $h^1(X,\mathcal{E}_b) \neq 0$ if and only if
		$h^0(C,L_1) \geq 1$ or $h^0(D,L_2) \geq 1$. Thus
		$$
		\operatorname{BN}^1_\pi=(W_1^0(C) \times \operatorname{Pic}^1(D)) \cup (\operatorname{Pic}^1(C) \times W_1^0(D)).
		$$
		Then
		$$
		\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{BN}_\pi^1)=g_1+g_2-\max\{g_1+1, g_2+1\}=\min\{g_1-1, g_2-1\} \geq 2=k-i+1.
		$$
		Theorem~\ref{cri} now shows that $R^2\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$ is torsion free.
<!-- end example-3 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
In fact, by Lemma \ref{l-codim-support-vanishing}, we know that under the codimension condition
		$$
		\operatorname{codim}_{B}(\operatorname{BN}_\pi^i) \geq k-i+1,
		$$ 
		$R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E})=0$ if and only if $R^i\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$ is torsion free. Thus if we have gotten $\pi_*\mathcal{E}$ is torsion free, to get $R^k\pi_*(\mathcal{E})$ is torsion free, we only need to check the codimention conditions for each $i \leq k-1$. This also provides another method to check the example above.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- example-4: body_md -->
Let $A$ be an abelian variety of dimension $g$ and $\hat{A}=\operatorname{Pic}^0(A)$ its dual.
		Let $M=A\times\hat{A}$, $\mathcal{P}$ its Poincar\'e line bundle, and
		$\pi:A\times\hat{A}\to\hat{A}$ the projection. When $\hat{x}\neq0$, we have
		$$
		h^i(A,\mathcal{P}_{\hat{x}})=0
		$$ 
		by \cite[(vii), p. 76]{Mum70}. When $\hat{x}=0$, we have
		$$
		h^i(A,\mathcal{P}_0)=h^i(A,\mathcal{O}_A)
		=\dim(\Lambda^i\mathbb{C}^g)=\binom{g}{i}.
		$$
		Thus, for all $i=0,1,\ldots,g$, we have
		$$
		\operatorname{BN}^i_{\pi}=\{0\}.
		$$
		Then 
		$$
		\operatorname{codim}(\operatorname{BN}_\pi^i)=g
		$$
		for all $0 \leq i \leq g$.
		The Fourier--Mukai transform \cite{Mukai81} gives
		$$
		\mathbb{R}\pi_*{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{O}_{\{0\}}[-g],
		$$
		and hence
		$$
		R^i\pi_*\mathcal{P}=0 \quad \text{for all $0\leq i<g$},
		\qquad R^g\pi_*\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{O}_{\{0\}}.
		$$
		Thus $R^g\pi_*\mathcal{P}$ is a torsion sheaf, showing that the
		codimension condition in the theorem is optimal.
<!-- end example-4 -->
<!-- theorem-15: body_md -->
\label{t-threefold-plurigenera}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a $\mathbb{Q}$-factorial K\"ahler family of compact threefolds over a relatively compact, smooth connected curve $S$. Assume that
		$X_0$ has canonical singularities. Then, for every integer $m\geq1$, the
		$m$-th plurigenus
		\[
		P_m(X_t)=\dim H^0(X_t,mK_{X_t})
		\]
		is independent of $t$ near $0$.
<!-- end theorem-15 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
The proof follows the strategy of \cite[Theorem~11]{Naka85}. By
		\cite[Theorem~1.2]{DHP23}, we can run a $K_X$-MMP over $S$. This MMP terminates with
		either a relative minimal model or a relative Mori fiber space. First,
		suppose that some step of the MMP contracts the central fiber. We claim that
		$P_m(X_0)=0$ for all $m\geq1$. Suppose that $X_0$ is contracted at the
		$i$-th step $\phi^i:X^{(i)}\dashrightarrow X^{(i+1)}/S$ of the MMP. This step must
		be a divisorial contraction because $X_0$ is a divisor. Then $-K_{X^{(i)}}$ is
		$\phi^i$-ample, so
		$H^0(X_0^{(i)},\mathcal O_{X_0^{(i)}}(mK_{X^{(i)}}))=0$ for all $m\geq1$. Since the
		composition of the first $i$ steps of the MMP does not contract $X_0$, the
		fibers $X_0$ and $X_0^{(i)}$ are bimeromorphic. Hence
		$P_m(X_0)=P_m(X_0^{(i)})=0$ for all $m\geq1$. Since $f$ is flat, upper
		semicontinuity of $h^0$ implies that $P_m(X_t)=0$ for every nearby $t$ and
		every $m\geq1$.
		
		Therefore, we may assume that the central fiber is not contracted by any step of the MMP throughout the rest of the proof, and we divide the proof into two cases.
		
		\noindent\textbf{Case 1:} The relative $K_X$-MMP terminates with a relative minimal model over $S$.
		\begin{center}
			\begin{tikzcd}[ampersand replacement=\&]
				X \&\& {X'} \\
				\& S
				\arrow["\phi", dashed, from=1-1, to=1-3]
				\arrow["f"', from=1-1, to=2-2]
				\arrow["{f'}", from=1-3, to=2-2]
			\end{tikzcd}
		\end{center}
		
		Since the MMP terminates with a relative minimal model, $K_{X'}$ is nef over
		$S$. By \cite{DO24,DO26}, $K_{X'}$ is semiample over $S$.
		Proposition~\ref{p-kollar-torsion freeness} shows that
		$R^if'_*\mathcal O_{X'}(mK_{X'})$ is locally free on $S$ for all $i\geq 0$
		and $m\geq 1$. By Theorem~\ref{Main}, the degree-zero base change map
		\[
		\varphi^0(t):
		(f'_*\mathcal{O}_{X'}(mK_{X'}))_t
		\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S,t}}\mathbb{C}(t)
		\longrightarrow
		H^0\!\left(X'_t,\mathcal{O}_{X'}(mK_{X'})|_{X'_t}\right)
		\]
		is surjective and hence an isomorphism. Moreover, by
		\cite[Corollary~6.2]{Naka87},
		\[
		\mathcal{O}_{X_t'}(mK_{X'}) \cong \mathcal{O}_{X_t'}(mK_{X_t'}),
		\qquad \text{for all } t \in S,\ m \ge 1.
		\]
		Therefore, by the local freeness of $f_*'\mathcal{O}_{X'}(mK_{X'})$, we have
		\[
		P_m(X_t') = \mathrm{rk}\, f_*'\mathcal{O}_{X'}(mK_{X'})(t)
		= \mathrm{rk}\, f_*'\mathcal{O}_{X'}(mK_{X'})(0)
		= P_m(X_0'),
		\qquad m \ge 1.
		\]
		It follows that $P_m(X_t)$ is also constant in the family for all $m \ge 1$.
		
		
		\noindent\textbf{Case 2:} The relative $K_X$-MMP terminates at a relative Mori fiber space $\psi : X' \to Z/S$, hence
		\[
		h^0\!\left(X_t',\, \mathcal{O}_{X_t'}(mK_{X'})\right) = 0
		\qquad \text{for all } m \ge 1.
		\]
		Thus $P_m(X_t')=0$ for all $m\geq1$. Since $X_t$ has canonical
		singularities and is bimeromorphic to $X_t'$ for $t$ near $0$, we have
		$P_m(X_t)=P_m(X_t')=0$ for every $m\geq1$ and every $t$ in a
		neighborhood of $0$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
Chen--Rao--Tsai~\cite[Theorem~4.7]{CRT25} established a
		criterion for fiberwise bimeromorphism under the assumption that
		$\kappa(X_0)\geq0$. In the MMP setting considered above, our argument gives
		a complementary statement: if fiberwise bimeromorphism fails over every
		neighborhood of $0$, then $P_m(X_0)=0$ for all $m\geq1$, and hence
		$\kappa(X_0)=-\infty$.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- theorem-16: body_md -->
\label{t-maxAlbdim}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a smooth K\"ahler family over a relatively compact,
		smooth, connected curve, and assume that $X_0$ has maximal Albanese
		dimension. Then, for every $m\geq1$, $P_m(X_t)$ is independent of $t$
		near $0$.
<!-- end theorem-16 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{p-maximal-albanese-cotangent}
		A compact K\"ahler manifold $X$ has maximal Albanese dimension if and
		only if its cotangent bundle $\Omega_X^1$ is generically generated by
		global sections; equivalently, the evaluation morphism
		\[
		H^0(X,\Omega_X^1)\otimes\mathcal O_X
		\longrightarrow\Omega_X^1
		\]
		is surjective at the generic point of $X$.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{l-Albdef}
		Let $f: X \to S$ be a smooth K\"ahler family over $S$. If the central fiber $X_0$ has maximal Albanese dimension, then so does every nearby fiber $X_t$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
By Ehresmann's theorem, $f:X\to S$ is locally trivial as a smooth fiber
		bundle and their first
		Betti number
		\[
		b_1(X_t):=\dim_{\mathbb Q}H^1(X_t,\mathbb Q)
		\]
		is locally constant in $t$. Since every fiber $X_t$ is a compact K\"ahler
		manifold, Hodge decomposition and Hodge symmetry give
		\[
		H^1(X_t,\mathbb C)
		\cong H^{1,0}(X_t)\oplus H^{0,1}(X_t)
		\cong H^0(X_t,\Omega^1_{X_t})\oplus H^1(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t})
		\]
		and
		\[
		\dim H^0(X_t,\Omega^1_{X_t})
		=\dim H^1(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t}).
		\]
		Consequently,
		\[
		q(X_t)=\dim H^1(X_t,\mathcal O_{X_t})=\frac{1}{2}b_1(X_t),
		\]
		so the irregularity is locally constant on $S$.
		
		We now prove that having maximal Albanese dimension is stable under deformation. Let
		$n=\dim X_t$ and let $q$ denote the
		common value of $q(X_t)$. Since $\dim H^0(X_t, \Omega_{X_t})$ is constant in $t$, by Grauert's theorem,
		\[
		\mathcal E:=f_*\Omega^1_{X/S}
		\]
		is locally free of rank $q$ on $S$, and commutes with base
		change. Consider the natural evaluation morphism
		\[
		\operatorname{ev}:f^*\mathcal E\longrightarrow\Omega^1_{X/S}.
		\]
		By \cite[Proposition~3.10]{HLR26}, its restriction to $X_t$ is
		\[
		\operatorname{ev}_t:
		H^0(X_t,\Omega^1_{X_t})\otimes\mathcal O_{X_t}
		\longrightarrow\Omega^1_{X_t}.
		\]
		Let
		\[
		W:=X\setminus\operatorname{Supp}\bigl(\operatorname{coker}(\operatorname{ev})\bigr).
		\]
		This is a Zariski open subset of $X$. Since $X_0$ has maximal Albanese dimension, Proposition~\ref{p-maximal-albanese-cotangent} implies that
		$\operatorname{ev}_0$ is generically surjective; hence
		$W\cap X_0\neq\varnothing$. The smooth morphism $f$ is open, so $f(W)$ is an
		open neighborhood of $0$ in $S$. After shrinking $S$ around $0$, we may
		therefore assume that $W\cap X_t\neq\varnothing$ for every $t\in S$. Thus
		$\operatorname{ev}_t$ is generically surjective on $X_t$.
		Therefore, by Proposition~\ref{p-maximal-albanese-cotangent}, every nearby fiber $X_t$ has maximal Albanese dimension.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{l-refnefalb}
		Let $(X,B)$ be a klt K\"ahler pair and $f:X\to S$ a surjective morphism
		of compact complex analytic varieties. Assume that $K_X+B$ is $f$-nef and
		$S$ contains no rational curves. Then $K_X+B$ is nef.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Suppose that $K_X+B$ is not nef. By the cone theorem
		(\cite[Theorem~0.5]{HP24} and also \cite[Corollary 3.6]{HLX26}), there exists a rational curve $C$ on $X$ such
		that $\left(K_X+B\right)\cdot C<0$. Since $K_X+B$ is $f$-nef, the image
		$f(C)$ is not a point. This contradicts the assumption that $S$ contains
		no rational curves. Therefore, $K_X+B$ is nef.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{l-existmmp}
		Let $f:X\to Y$ be a contraction morphism of normal compact complex
		varieties. Assume that $(X,B+\boldsymbol{\beta})$ is a generalized klt pair,
		that $B+\boldsymbol{\beta}_X$ is modified big over $Y$, and that
		$\boldsymbol{\beta}\equiv_Y\mathbf N$, where $\mathbf N$ is an
		$\mathbb R$-Cartier $b$-divisor.
		\begin{enumerate}
			\item[(1)] If $K_X+B+\boldsymbol{\beta}_X$ is pseudoeffective over $Y$,
			then $(X,B+\boldsymbol{\beta})$ has a good minimal model over $Y$, not
			necessarily $\mathbb Q$-factorial.
			\item[(2)] If $K_X+B+\boldsymbol{\beta}_X$ is not pseudoeffective over
			$Y$, then $(X,B+\boldsymbol{\beta})$ is birational to a Fano fibration
			over $Y$.
		\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- theorem-17: body_md -->
\label{Existencegoodmm}
		Let $f:X\to S$ be a smooth K\"ahler family over a relative compact, smooth connected curve $S$ such that $X_0$ has maximal Albanese dimension. Then, after shrinking $S$ around $0$, the family admits a relative good minimal model.
<!-- end theorem-17 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{l-Albdef}, maximal Albanese dimension is preserved under small deformations. Hence, after shrinking $S$ around $0$, every fiber $X_t$ has maximal Albanese dimension. Let
		\[
		\alpha : X \longrightarrow \mathcal A := \operatorname{Alb}(X/S)
		\]
		be the relative Albanese map, which is generically finite, and write
		$\alpha = g\circ\beta$ for its Stein factorization, with $\beta : X \to \mathcal A^{\mathrm{st}}$
		bimeromorphic and $g$ finite. Since $g$ is finite, a class is nef, semiample, or big
		over $\mathcal A^{\mathrm{st}}$ if and only if it is so over $\mathcal A$, so we may work over
		$\mathcal A^{\mathrm{st}}$ throughout. By Lemma \ref{l-existmmp}, a $K_X$-good minimal
		model over $\mathcal A$ exists,
		\begin{center}
			\begin{tikzcd}
				X && Y \\
				& {\mathcal{A}} \\
				& S
				\arrow[dashed, from=1-1, to=1-3]
				\arrow["\alpha", from=1-1, to=2-2]
				\arrow["f"', from=1-1, to=3-2]
				\arrow["{\alpha'}"', from=1-3, to=2-2]
				\arrow["{f'}", from=1-3, to=3-2]
				\arrow[from=2-2, to=3-2]
			\end{tikzcd}
		\end{center}
		where $Y$ is strongly $\mathbb Q$-factorial and klt, and $K_Y$ is
		semiample over $\mathcal A$, with induced morphisms $\alpha':Y\to\mathcal A$ and $f':Y\to S$.
		
		We claim that $K_Y$ is semi-ample over $S$. Since $K_Y$ is nef over
		$\mathcal A$ and the fibers $\mathcal A_t$ are complex tori containing no rational curves,
		Lemma \ref{l-refnefalb} shows that $K_Y$ is nef over $S$. Hence for general $t\in S$ the induced
		map $X_t\dashrightarrow Y_t$ is a minimal model of $X_t$. By \cite[Theorem~1.1]{DHAlbanese}, the fibers $X_t$
		admit good minimal models, so $K_{Y_t}$ is semiample for general $t$. By the base point free theorem
		\cite[Theorem~5.8]{Naka87}, $K_Y$ is semiample over $S$, so that $X\dashrightarrow Y$ is a
		relative good minimal model of $X$ over $S$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The proof is very similar to that of Theorem~\ref{t-threefold-plurigenera}. By Theorem~\ref{Existencegoodmm}, we may run a relative $K_X$-MMP over $S$,
		\[
		X\dashrightarrow X'/S,
		\]
		which terminates with a relative good minimal model such that $K_{X'}$ is semiample over $S$. By Proposition~\ref{p-kollar-torsion freeness}, $R^i f'_*\mathcal{O}_{X'}(mK_{X'})$ is locally free for every $i\geq 0$ and $m\ge 1$. Therefore, the plurigenera $P_m(X_t)$ are constant in the family.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- theorem-18: body_md -->
\label{t-log-plurigenera}
		Let $g:X\to S$ be a smooth family such that $(X,B)$ is log smooth over a relatively compact, smooth connected curve $S$. Suppose that the special fiber $(X_0,B_0)$ is projective with canonical singularities, that $\lfloor B\rfloor=0$, and that $K_{X_0}+B_0$ is big.
		Assume moreover that the negative part of its Boucksom--Zariski
		decomposition satisfies
		$$
		N(K_{X_0}+B_0)\wedge B_0 = 0 .
		$$
		Then there exists an open neighborhood $U\subset S$ of $0$ such that,
		for every integer $m\geq1$ such that $mB$ is integral, the $m$-th log plurigenus
		$$
		P_m(X_t,B_t)=h^{0}\!\left(X_t,\, m(K_{X_t}+B_t)\right)
		$$
		is independent of $t\in U$.
<!-- end theorem-18 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Our proof is motivated by \cite[Theorem~1]{DefGeneralType}. Since
		$(X_0,B_0)$ is projective, \cite{BCHM} allows us to run the
		$(K_{X_0}+B_0)$-MMP with scaling, which terminates with a minimal model:
		\[
		(X_0,B_0)\dashrightarrow(X_0^{(1)},B_0^{(1)})\dashrightarrow\cdots
		\dashrightarrow(X_0',B_0').
		\]
		By Theorem~\ref{ExtMMP}, this MMP extends over a connected neighborhood
		$U\subset S$ to a sequence of $(K_X+B)$-negative bimeromorphic contractions $$(X,B)\dashrightarrow(X^{(1)},B^{(1)})\dashrightarrow\cdots
		\dashrightarrow(X',B')/U,$$denote $\pi' : X' \to U$. Since
		$K_{X_0'}+B_0'$ is nef and big and $(X_0',B_0')$ is klt, so fix $i>0$
		and $m\ge 2$ such that $mB$ is integral. By Kawamata--Viehweg
		vanishing,
		$H^i\!\left(X_0',
		\mathcal{O}_{X_0'}\bigl(m(K_{X_0'}+B_0')\bigr)\right)=0$. Then the
		upper semicontinuity of cohomological dimension gives $$
		H^i\!\left(X_t',
		\mathcal O_{X_t'}\bigl(m(K_{X_t'}+B_t')\bigr)\right)=0,$$
		for every nearby $t$. In particular, $h^1(X_t',\mathcal O(m(K_{X_t'}+B_t')))=0$ for $t$ near $0$. Since cohomology in degree $-1$ is also zero,
		Corollary~\ref{l-squeeze}, applied with $q=0$, shows that
		\[
		P_m(X_t',B_t')=h^0\!\left(X_t',
		\mathcal O_{X_t'}\bigl(m(K_{X_t'}+B_t')\bigr)\right)
		\]
		is locally constant in $t\in U_m$ for some open neighborhood $0\in U_m$, and the degree $0$ base change map $\varphi^{0}_m (0):\pi_*'\mathcal O_{X'}\bigl(m(K_{X'}+B')\bigr)(0)\to H^0\!\left(X_0',
		\mathcal O_{X_0'}\bigl(m(K_{X_0'}+B_0')\bigr)\right)$ is an isomorphism for all $m\ge 2$ with $mB$ integral.
		
		A priori the neighborhood $U_m$ could shrink to $\{0\}$ as $m$ grows.
		We show that the same neighborhood works for all $m$. Set
		$L=\mathcal{O}_{X'}(N(K_{X'}+B'))$, where $N\ge 2$ is chosen so that
		$N(K_{X'}+B')$ is Cartier. By our discussion,
		$\varphi_{\ell N}^{(0)}(0)$ is an isomorphism for every $\ell\ge 1$;
		we thus have the isomorphism
		$$\bigoplus_{\ell\ge0}(\pi_*'L^\ell)\otimes k(0)\;\cong\;
		\bigoplus_{\ell\ge0}H^0\bigl(X'_0,\,
		\ell N(K_{X'_0}+B'_0)\bigr),$$
		where $L^\ell:=\mathcal O_{X'}(\ell N(K_{X'}+B'))$. On the other hand,
		since $K_{X_0'}+B_0'$ is semi-ample, the right-hand side is finitely
		generated. Then, by \cite[Lemma 11]{DefGeneralType}, there exists a
		neighborhood $0\in U\subset S$, independent of $m$, on which
		$\bigoplus_\ell\pi_*'L^\ell$ is a finitely generated
		$\mathcal O_U$-algebra. We then take
		$$Z:=\operatorname{Proj}_U\bigoplus_{\ell\ge0}\pi_*'L^\ell
		\longrightarrow U,$$ with $K_Z+B_Z$ ample over $U$ and an associated
		contraction $\varphi:X'\to Z$ such that
		$\varphi^*(K_Z+B_Z)=K_{X'}+B'$. For every $m\ge2$ such that $mB$ is
		integral, Kawamata--Viehweg vanishing gives
		\[
		H^i\bigl(Z_t,\mathcal O_{Z_t}(m(K_{Z_t}+B_{Z_t}))\bigr)=0
		\qquad (i>0,\ t\in U),
		\]
		where $U$ is independent of $m$. Since the cohomology in degree $-1$
		also vanishes, Corollary~\ref{l-squeeze}, applied with $q=0$, shows
		that $P_m(Z_t,B_{Z_t})$ is constant on $U$. Hence $P_m(X_t',B_t')$ is constant on $U$ as
		well.
		
		By \cite[Lemma~2.22]{CRT25}, after
		shrinking $U$ if necessary, the birational map $X\dashrightarrow X'$
		is fiberwise bimeromorphic; in particular, $X_t$ and $X_t'$ are
		bimeromorphic for every $t$ near $0$. Since the MMP is $(K_X+B)$-negative, the log plurigenera are preserved at each
		step. Consequently,
		\[
		P_m(X_t,B_t)=\dim H^0\!\left(X_t,
		\mathcal O_{X_t}\bigl(m(K_{X_t}+B_t)\bigr)\right)
		\]
		is locally constant in $t$ near $0$.
		
		It remains to consider the case $m=1$. In this case, by assumption $B$ is integral; since $\lfloor B\rfloor=0$, we have $B=0$. Serre duality gives
		\[
		h^0\!\left(X_t,\mathcal{O}_{X_t}(K_{X_t})\right)
		=h^n\!\left(X_t,\mathcal{O}_{X_t}\right), 
		\]where $n = \dim X_t$.
		The result then follows from \cite[Complement 2.62.5]{Kolbook}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- theorem-19: body_md -->
\label{Exthigherdeg}
		\leavevmode
		Let $X$ be a complex manifold and $(L,h)$ a holomorphic line
		bundle on $X$ endowed with a possibly singular Hermitian metric.
		\begin{itemize}
			\item[(1)]\phantomsection\label{Exthigherdeg-part-1} Let $\psi$ be a quasi-plurisubharmonic function with neat
			analytic singularities and assume that $X$ is holomorphically convex and
			K\"ahler. Suppose that there is a positive continuous function
			$\delta$ on $X$ such that
			\[
			\sqrt{-1}\Theta_{L,h}
			+(1+\alpha\delta)\sqrt{-1}\partial\bar\partial\psi\geq0
			\qquad\text{for every }\alpha\in[0,1].
			\]
			Then the natural inclusion
			$\mathcal I(he^{-\psi})\hookrightarrow\mathcal I(h)$ induces an
			injective map
			\[
			H^q\!\left(X,\mathcal O_X(K_X+L)\otimes\mathcal I(he^{-\psi})\right)
			\longrightarrow
			H^q\!\left(X,\mathcal O_X(K_X+L)\otimes\mathcal I(h)\right)
			\]
			for every $q\geq0$. Equivalently, for every $q\geq0$, the quotient
			map induces a surjection
			\[
			H^q\!\left(X,\mathcal O_X(K_X+L)\otimes\mathcal I(h)\right)
			\longrightarrow
			H^q\!\left(X,\mathcal O_X(K_X+L)\otimes
			\frac{\mathcal I(h)}{\mathcal I(he^{-\psi})}\right).
			\]
			
			\item[(2)]\phantomsection\label{Exthigherdeg-part-2} Suppose that $f:X\to S$ is a surjective proper K\"ahler
			morphism to an analytic space and that $h$ has semipositive curvature.
			Then
			\[
			R^qf_*\!\left(\mathcal O_X(K_X+L)\otimes\mathcal I(h)\right)
			\]
			is torsion free for every $q\geq0$, and it vanishes for
			$q>\dim X-\dim S$.
			
			\item[(3)]\phantomsection\label{Exthigherdeg-part-3} Assume, in addition to the hypotheses of
			part~\hyperref[Exthigherdeg-part-2]{(2)}, that
			$S$ is a smooth curve, $f$ is smooth, and $h$ is smooth. For any
